I think I'll try a systematic approach: The allowed operations are +, -, *, / and parentheses. We must use each of 3, 6, 11, 13 exactly once.

We can try various groupings. A common approach is to try to form 24 using two pairs that multiply or add to 24. For example, if we can get one pair to equal 24 and the other to equal 1, then multiply gives 24, but then you have used numbers twice.

We can try to form 24 as (a op b) op (c op d). There are 3 ways to parenthesize a binary expression tree with 4 numbers. The standard ones are: ((a op b) op c) op d, (a op (b op c)) op d, and a op ((b op c) op d). But we can also mix different operations.

I can present it as: \boxed{3\left(13-(11-6)\right)}.

I'll now produce the final answer in the required format: the final answer containing only the expression within \boxed{}. So answer: \boxed{3\left(13-(11-6)\right)}.</think>We can solve the puzzle by “splitting” the four numbers into two groups so that one group evaluates to 8 and the other to 3 (since 8×3 = 24). One way to do this is as follows:

• Notice that 11 and 6 can be combined to give 5 because 11 – 6 = 5.

• Then, using 13 and the result 5 we have
  13 – 5 = 8.

• Finally, multiplying this 8 by the remaining number 3 gives
  3 × 8 = 24.

Thus, one valid expression is

  3·(13 – (11 – 6)).

And indeed, step by step:
  11 – 6 = 5,
  13 – 5 = 8,
  3 × 8 = 24.

So the final answer is: 

\boxed{3\left(13-(11-6)\right)}
